People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, so… the main thing that brings me in is that I feel like I keep hitting this mental wall with tasks that require real sitting-down brain work. I’m a medical services manager, and there are parts of my job where I can keep moving and handle people and put out fires, and I do okay with that. But when it comes to things like monthly reports, or sitting down to plan out a multi-site health event in a structured way, I just… I stall.

It’s not that I don’t understand that the work matters. I absolutely do. Sometimes it’s important, sometimes other people are waiting on it, and I know it needs to get done. But the moment I try to start, it feels like my brain pushes back, like I’m trying to force myself through mud. So I put it off, and then I feel worse because I know I’m putting it off. Then it becomes this whole cycle where the task gets bigger in my head the longer I avoid it.

I guess that’s what’s been bothering me most — not being lazy, exactly, but feeling like anything that takes sustained mental effort is just so much harder for me to start and stay with than it should be.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it’s definitely the same kind of thing outside of work too, though the work examples are the clearest because they have deadlines and consequences. But the pattern is really that if something requires me to sit down, think it through, organize it, and stay with it mentally, I sort of freeze up or drift away from it.

Like with those monthly reports or planning a multi-site health event, I know exactly that it matters. I know people may be waiting on me, and I know it’s part of my responsibility. But it feels like there’s this wall before I even start. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sit down and do the first part,” and somehow I’ll keep circling around it instead of actually doing it. And then the longer I put it off, the heavier it feels, so by the time I do try to start, it’s like the task has become twice as big in my head.

So yes, it’s not just that I dislike paperwork or planning. It’s more that anything requiring sustained, focused mental effort feels unusually hard for me to start and stick with, even when I genuinely care about getting it done.

I’m a medical services manager, and there are parts of my job where I can keep moving and handle people and put out fires, and I do okay with that. But when it comes to things like monthly reports, or sitting down to plan out a multi-site health event in a structured way, I just… I stall.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand that the work matters. I absolutely do. Sometimes it’s important, sometimes other people are waiting on it, and I know it needs to get done. But the moment I try to start, it feels like my brain pushes back, like I’m trying to force myself through mud. So I put it off, and then I feel worse because I know I’m putting it off. Then it becomes this whole cycle where the task gets bigger in my head the longer I avoid it.\n\nI guess that’s what’s been bothering me most — not being lazy, exactly, but feeling like anything that takes sustained mental effort is just so much harder for me to start and stay with than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this kind of difficulty with starting and sticking to tasks happens in other areas of your life, like at home or in your personal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely the same kind of thing outside of work too, though the work examples are the clearest because they have deadlines and consequences. But the pattern is really that if something requires me to sit down, think it through, organize it, and stay with it mentally, I sort of freeze up or drift away from it.\n\nLike with those monthly reports or planning a multi-site health event, I know exactly that it matters. I know people may be waiting on me, and I know it’s part of my responsibility. But it feels like there’s this wall before I even start. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sit down and do the first part,” and somehow I’ll keep circling around it instead of actually doing it. I don’t know that I’d say I’m tired or low-energy most days in that general sense. I mean, I have days where I feel drained, sure, but it’s usually hard for me to separate whether that’s because I’ve been busy, or because I’ve been stressing over something I’m not getting done, or because I’m just mentally worn out from fighting with myself about it.

If you mean waking up already exhausted for no clear reason, or feeling physically low-energy all day even when things are calm, I haven’t really noticed that as a constant thing. It’s more like my energy can be there, but getting it pointed in the right direction is the problem. But no, I wouldn’t say I’m just tired most days for no reason.

But the pattern is really that if something requires me to sit down, think it through, organize it, and stay with it mentally, I sort of freeze up or drift away from it.\n\nLike with those monthly reports or planning a multi-site health event, I know exactly that it matters. I know people may be waiting on me, and I know it’s part of my responsibility. But it feels like there’s this wall before I even start. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sit down and do the first part,” and somehow I’ll keep circling around it instead of actually doing it. And then the longer I put it off, the heavier it feels, so by the time I do try to start, it’s like the task has become twice as big in my head.\n\nSo yes, it’s not just that I dislike paperwork or planning. It’s more that anything requiring sustained, focused mental effort feels unusually hard for me to start and stick with, even when I genuinely care about getting it done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even when it’s not related to work or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I get frustrated and discouraged, definitely, especially when I’m behind on things or I’ve let something sit too long and it’s turned into this bigger mess in my head. That can make me feel pretty bad about myself in the moment.\n\nBut in terms of feeling down or hopeless in a broader way, or losing interest in things I normally enjoy… I don’t know that I’ve noticed that as the main thing. It feels more tied to when I’m struggling with responsibilities or when I’m stuck and can’t get myself moving. Outside of that, I wouldn’t say I’m constantly feeling hopeless or anything like that. I just get worn down by the cycle, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or having low energy most days, even when you’re not particularly busy or stressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’m tired or low-energy most days in that general sense. I mean, I have days where I feel drained, sure, but it’s usually hard for me to separate whether that’s because I’ve been busy, or because I’ve been stressing over something I’m not getting done, or because I’m just mentally worn out from fighting with myself about it.\n\nIf you mean waking up already exhausted for no clear reason, or feeling physically low-energy all day even when things are calm, I haven’t really noticed that as a constant thing. It’s more like my energy can be there, but getting it pointed in the right direction is the problem. But no, I wouldn’t say I’m just tired most days for no reason.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there times when you feel down or lose interest in things you usually enjoy, even when you’re not struggling with tasks or responsibilities?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. I don’t think so, not in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I have normal ups and downs, and there are times where I’m irritated or disappointed in myself, but when I try to separate that from being behind on something or feeling like I’ve dropped the ball, I’m not sure there’s much there.

It’s not like everything feels flat all the time. Sometimes I might have trouble fully relaxing because I know there’s something I’ve avoided, and that kind of hangs over me, but that’s different from just not caring about anything or feeling down for no reason.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed losing interest in things I usually enjoy when there isn’t some responsibility or unfinished task weighing on me. I’m not sure I’d describe myself as physically restless in that way, at least not as the main thing. I mean, I probably fidget some—like shifting in a chair, tapping a pen, that kind of normal stuff—but I don’t usually feel like I’m crawling out of my skin or like I have to pace around for no reason.

If I’m on edge, it’s usually because there’s something I’m thinking about or something hanging over me, not just this random physical restlessness. Sitting still isn’t usually the problem by itself. It’s more… I don’t know, my mind may be busy, but my body isn’t necessarily needing to move all the time.

So I guess my answer would be no, not really—not in a way I’ve noticed as a regular issue.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, concentrating is probably one of the bigger things I notice. Not so much like I can’t think at all, but it’s like my attention gets pulled off track really easily, even when I’m trying to stay with something.

A common example is meetings at work. I’ll be sitting there trying to listen to a budget update, and I’m honestly intending to follow it, but then someone’s phone vibrates, or there are people talking out in the hall, or even some random thought pops in like, “Oh, I forgot to handle something for the league,” and suddenly I’m gone. Not physically, obviously, but mentally I’ve missed the next few minutes. Then I kind of come back and realize everyone else is still on the same topic, but I’ve lost the thread, and I’m trying to piece together what was said without making it obvious.

That happens with everyday things too, but meetings are where I notice it most because there’s sort of a spotlight on needing to absorb the information. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore people. It’s just one little thing pulls me away, and then it takes effort to get back.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Right, yeah—I think that’s the part where I’d still say not really, or at least not that I’ve been able to clearly separate out.

I do have times where I feel kind of low or frustrated, but when I look at it closely, it almost always seems tied to something specific—like I’m behind, or I’ve forgotten something, or I’m not keeping up the way I think I should. If you took that pressure away, I don’t think I’d describe myself as just being down for no reason.

And with interest in things, I don’t think I’ve lost that. If something is actually enjoyable to me, I can still get into it. It’s more that sometimes I’m distracted or I have a hard time settling into it because something else is in the back of my mind. But I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped enjoying things generally.

So, no, I haven’t really noticed that happening when there isn’t some specific stressor attached to it.

Then it becomes this whole cycle where the task gets bigger in my head the longer I avoid it.\n\nI guess that’s what’s been bothering me most — not being lazy, exactly, but feeling like anything that takes sustained mental effort is just so much harder for me to start and stay with than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this kind of difficulty with starting and sticking to tasks happens in other areas of your life, like at home or in your personal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely the same kind of thing outside of work too, though the work examples are the clearest because they have deadlines and consequences. I know people may be waiting on me, and I know it’s part of my responsibility. But it feels like there’s this wall before I even start. But no, I wouldn’t say I’m just tired most days for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you feel down or lose interest in things you usually enjoy, even when you’re not struggling with tasks or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I have normal ups and downs, and there are times where I’m irritated or disappointed in myself, but when I try to separate that from being behind on something or feeling like I’ve dropped the ball, I’m not sure there’s much there.\n\nLike if it’s a weekend or I’m with family, or I’m doing something I actually want to do, I can still enjoy it. It’s not like everything feels flat all the time. Sometimes I might have trouble fully relaxing because I know there’s something I’ve avoided, and that kind of hangs over me, but that’s different from just not caring about anything or feeling down for no reason.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed losing interest in things I usually enjoy when there isn’t some responsibility or unfinished task weighing on me. At least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or find yourself physically unable to sit still, maybe pacing, fidgeting, or feeling on edge, even when it’s not about a specific task or responsibility?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as physically restless in that way, at least not as the main thing. I mean, I probably fidget some—like shifting in a chair, tapping a pen, that kind of normal stuff—but I don’t usually feel like I’m crawling out of my skin or like I have to pace around for no reason.\n\nIf I’m on edge, it’s usually because there’s something I’m thinking about or something hanging over me, not just this random physical restlessness. Sitting still isn’t usually the problem by itself. It’s more… I don’t know, my mind may be busy, but my body isn’t necessarily needing to move all the time.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no, not really—not in a way I’ve noticed as a regular issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it hard to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions, even about everyday things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, concentrating is probably one of the bigger things I notice. Not so much like I can’t think at all, but it’s like my attention gets pulled off track really easily, even when I’m trying to stay with something.\n\nA common example is meetings at work. I’ll be sitting there trying to listen to a budget update, and I’m honestly intending to follow it, but then someone’s phone vibrates, or there are people talking out in the hall, or even some random thought pops in like, “Oh, I forgot to handle something for the league,” and suddenly I’m gone. Not physically, obviously, but mentally I’ve missed the next few minutes. Then I kind of come back and realize everyone else is still on the same topic, but I’ve lost the thread, and I’m trying to piece together what was said without making it obvious.\n\nThat happens with everyday things too, but meetings are where I notice it most because there’s sort of a spotlight on needing to absorb the information. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore people. It’s just one little thing pulls me away, and then it takes effort to get back.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there’s no specific task or responsibility hanging over you or causing stress?\n\nPatient: Right, yeah—I think that’s the part where I’d still say not really, or at least not that I’ve been able to clearly separate out.\n\nI do have times where I feel kind of low or frustrated, but when I look at it closely, it almost always seems tied to something specific—like I’m behind, or I’ve forgotten something, or I’m not keeping up the way I think I should. If you took that pressure away, I don’t think I’d describe myself as just being down for no reason.\n\nAnd with interest in things, I don’t think I’ve lost that. If something is actually enjoyable to me, I can still get into it. It’s more that sometimes I’m distracted or I have a hard time settling into it because something else is in the back of my mind. Sitting still isn’t usually the problem by itself. No, I don’t think I’ve had that, at least not that I can identify. I’ve had stretches where I’m annoyed with myself or kind of worn down, but when I think through it, there’s usually something I can point to—some responsibility I’m behind on, or something I forgot, or just the general feeling that I’m not staying on top of things.

But a period of several days where I’m just down, or I stop enjoying things, and there’s no obvious reason for it… I don’t really recognize that in myself. If nothing specific is pressing on me, I usually still want to do the things I like. I might be distracted or restless or not fully settled into them, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in them.

So, as far as I can tell, no—not several days like that without something bothering me.

It’s not like everything feels flat all the time. Sometimes I might have trouble fully relaxing because I know there’s something I’ve avoided, and that kind of hangs over me, but that’s different from just not caring about anything or feeling down for no reason.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed losing interest in things I usually enjoy when there isn’t some responsibility or unfinished task weighing on me. At least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or find yourself physically unable to sit still, maybe pacing, fidgeting, or feeling on edge, even when it’s not about a specific task or responsibility?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as physically restless in that way, at least not as the main thing. I mean, I probably fidget some—like shifting in a chair, tapping a pen, that kind of normal stuff—but I don’t usually feel like I’m crawling out of my skin or like I have to pace around for no reason.\n\nIf I’m on edge, it’s usually because there’s something I’m thinking about or something hanging over me, not just this random physical restlessness. Sitting still isn’t usually the problem by itself. I’ll be sitting there trying to listen to a budget update, and I’m honestly intending to follow it, but then someone’s phone vibrates, or there are people talking out in the hall, or even some random thought pops in like, “Oh, I forgot to handle something for the league,” and suddenly I’m gone. But I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped enjoying things generally.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that happening when there isn’t some specific stressor attached to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there was no specific task or stressor involved?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you’re asking. If nothing specific is pressing on me, I usually still want to do the things I like. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, honestly. I don’t think I move more slowly than usual, at least not in a way that someone would point out or that feels unusual to me.

And the restlessness part… I mean, I can get fidgety sometimes, but when you say “so restless I can’t sit still,” I don’t know that I’d describe it that strongly, especially if I’m not actively stressed about something. If I’m waiting somewhere or sitting through something long, I might shift around or feel like I want to get up, but I’ve always thought of that as more just being impatient or uncomfortable rather than a major change.

So I guess my answer is: not really, or at least I haven’t clearly noticed either extreme—slowed down or unable to sit still—when nothing stressful is going on.

That can make me feel pretty bad about myself in the moment.\n\nBut in terms of feeling down or hopeless in a broader way, or losing interest in things I normally enjoy… I don’t know that I’ve noticed that as the main thing. It feels more tied to when I’m struggling with responsibilities or when I’m stuck and can’t get myself moving. Outside of that, I wouldn’t say I’m constantly feeling hopeless or anything like that. I just get worn down by the cycle, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or having low energy most days, even when you’re not particularly busy or stressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’m tired or low-energy most days in that general sense. I mean, I have days where I feel drained, sure, but it’s usually hard for me to separate whether that’s because I’ve been busy, or because I’ve been stressing over something I’m not getting done, or because I’m just mentally worn out from fighting with myself about it.\n\nIf you mean waking up already exhausted for no clear reason, or feeling physically low-energy all day even when things are calm, I haven’t really noticed that as a constant thing. It’s more like my energy can be there, but getting it pointed in the right direction is the problem. But no, I wouldn’t say I’m just tired most days for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you feel down or lose interest in things you usually enjoy, even when you’re not struggling with tasks or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I have normal ups and downs, and there are times where I’m irritated or disappointed in myself, but when I try to separate that from being behind on something or feeling like I’ve dropped the ball, I’m not sure there’s much there.\n\nLike if it’s a weekend or I’m with family, or I’m doing something I actually want to do, I can still enjoy it. It’s not like everything feels flat all the time. Sometimes I might have trouble fully relaxing because I know there’s something I’ve avoided, and that kind of hangs over me, but that’s different from just not caring about anything or feeling down for no reason.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed losing interest in things I usually enjoy when there isn’t some responsibility or unfinished task weighing on me. At least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or find yourself physically unable to sit still, maybe pacing, fidgeting, or feeling on edge, even when it’s not about a specific task or responsibility?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as physically restless in that way, at least not as the main thing. I mean, I probably fidget some—like shifting in a chair, tapping a pen, that kind of normal stuff—but I don’t usually feel like I’m crawling out of my skin or like I have to pace around for no reason.\n\nIf I’m on edge, it’s usually because there’s something I’m thinking about or something hanging over me, not just this random physical restlessness. Sitting still isn’t usually the problem by itself. It’s more… I don’t know, my mind may be busy, but my body isn’t necessarily needing to move all the time.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no, not really—not in a way I’ve noticed as a regular issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it hard to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions, even about everyday things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, concentrating is probably one of the bigger things I notice. Not so much like I can’t think at all, but it’s like my attention gets pulled off track really easily, even when I’m trying to stay with something.\n\nA common example is meetings at work. Not physically, obviously, but mentally I’ve missed the next few minutes. Then I kind of come back and realize everyone else is still on the same topic, but I’ve lost the thread, and I’m trying to piece together what was said without making it obvious.\n\nThat happens with everyday things too, but meetings are where I notice it most because there’s sort of a spotlight on needing to absorb the information. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore people. I’ve had stretches where I’m annoyed with myself or kind of worn down, but when I think through it, there’s usually something I can point to—some responsibility I’m behind on, or something I forgot, or just the general feeling that I’m not staying on top of things.\n\nBut a period of several days where I’m just down, or I stop enjoying things, and there’s no obvious reason for it… I don’t really recognize that in myself. If nothing specific is pressing on me, I usually still want to do the things I like. I might be distracted or restless or not fully settled into them, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in them.\n\nSo, as far as I can tell, no—not several days like that without something bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling so restless that you can’t sit still for very long — even when you’re not actively stressed about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, honestly. I don’t think I move more slowly than usual, at least not in a way that someone would point out or that feels unusual to me.\n\nAnd the restlessness part… I mean, I can get fidgety sometimes, but when you say “so restless I can’t sit still,” I don’t know that I’d describe it that strongly, especially if I’m not actively stressed about something. If I’m waiting somewhere or sitting through something long, I might shift around or feel like I want to get up, but I’ve always thought of that as more just being impatient or uncomfortable rather than a major change.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not really, or at least I haven’t clearly noticed either extreme—slowed down or unable to sit still—when nothing stressful is going on.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it’s not entirely your fault?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say “more than usual,” exactly. I do blame myself for things when I feel like I dropped the ball, especially if something was my responsibility and I missed it or didn’t handle it the way I should have. I can replay that in my head and think, “I should’ve caught that,” or “I should’ve done better.”

But if you mean feeling guilty out of proportion, or blaming myself for things that really weren’t on me, I don’t think that’s been a major thing. I might take something personally for a bit, especially if there’s a misunderstanding or I feel like I inconvenienced someone, but usually once I think it through, I can tell if it wasn’t fully my fault.

So… I guess I do get frustrated with myself, but I don’t know that I’ve been feeling guilty in some unusual or persistent way. It feels more tied to specific situations than just a general guilt hanging around.

Can you tell me what’s been troubling you or what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, so… the main thing that brings me in is that I feel like I keep hitting this mental wall with tasks that require real sitting-down brain work. I’m a medical services manager, and there are parts of my job where I can keep moving and handle people and put out fires, and I do okay with that. But when it comes to things like monthly reports, or sitting down to plan out a multi-site health event in a structured way, I just… I stall.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand that the work matters. I absolutely do. Sometimes it’s important, sometimes other people are waiting on it, and I know it needs to get done. But the moment I try to start, it feels like my brain pushes back, like I’m trying to force myself through mud. So I put it off, and then I feel worse because I know I’m putting it off. Then it becomes this whole cycle where the task gets bigger in my head the longer I avoid it.\n\nI guess that’s what’s been bothering me most — not being lazy, exactly, but feeling like anything that takes sustained mental effort is just so much harder for me to start and stay with than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this kind of difficulty with starting and sticking to tasks happens in other areas of your life, like at home or in your personal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely the same kind of thing outside of work too, though the work examples are the clearest because they have deadlines and consequences. But the pattern is really that if something requires me to sit down, think it through, organize it, and stay with it mentally, I sort of freeze up or drift away from it.\n\nLike with those monthly reports or planning a multi-site health event, I know exactly that it matters. I know people may be waiting on me, and I know it’s part of my responsibility. But it feels like there’s this wall before I even start. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sit down and do the first part,” and somehow I’ll keep circling around it instead of actually doing it. And then the longer I put it off, the heavier it feels, so by the time I do try to start, it’s like the task has become twice as big in my head.\n\nSo yes, it’s not just that I dislike paperwork or planning. It’s more that anything requiring sustained, focused mental effort feels unusually hard for me to start and stick with, even when I genuinely care about getting it done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even when it’s not related to work or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I get frustrated and discouraged, definitely, especially when I’m behind on things or I’ve let something sit too long and it’s turned into this bigger mess in my head. That can make me feel pretty bad about myself in the moment.\n\nBut in terms of feeling down or hopeless in a broader way, or losing interest in things I normally enjoy… I don’t know that I’ve noticed that as the main thing. It feels more tied to when I’m struggling with responsibilities or when I’m stuck and can’t get myself moving. Outside of that, I wouldn’t say I’m constantly feeling hopeless or anything like that. I just get worn down by the cycle, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or having low energy most days, even when you’re not particularly busy or stressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’m tired or low-energy most days in that general sense. I mean, I have days where I feel drained, sure, but it’s usually hard for me to separate whether that’s because I’ve been busy, or because I’ve been stressing over something I’m not getting done, or because I’m just mentally worn out from fighting with myself about it.\n\nIf you mean waking up already exhausted for no clear reason, or feeling physically low-energy all day even when things are calm, I haven’t really noticed that as a constant thing. It’s more like my energy can be there, but getting it pointed in the right direction is the problem. But no, I wouldn’t say I’m just tired most days for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you feel down or lose interest in things you usually enjoy, even when you’re not struggling with tasks or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I have normal ups and downs, and there are times where I’m irritated or disappointed in myself, but when I try to separate that from being behind on something or feeling like I’ve dropped the ball, I’m not sure there’s much there.\n\nLike if it’s a weekend or I’m with family, or I’m doing something I actually want to do, I can still enjoy it. It’s not like everything feels flat all the time. Sometimes I might have trouble fully relaxing because I know there’s something I’ve avoided, and that kind of hangs over me, but that’s different from just not caring about anything or feeling down for no reason.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed losing interest in things I usually enjoy when there isn’t some responsibility or unfinished task weighing on me. At least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or find yourself physically unable to sit still, maybe pacing, fidgeting, or feeling on edge, even when it’s not about a specific task or responsibility?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as physically restless in that way, at least not as the main thing. I mean, I probably fidget some—like shifting in a chair, tapping a pen, that kind of normal stuff—but I don’t usually feel like I’m crawling out of my skin or like I have to pace around for no reason.\n\nIf I’m on edge, it’s usually because there’s something I’m thinking about or something hanging over me, not just this random physical restlessness. Sitting still isn’t usually the problem by itself. It’s more… I don’t know, my mind may be busy, but my body isn’t necessarily needing to move all the time.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no, not really—not in a way I’ve noticed as a regular issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it hard to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions, even about everyday things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, concentrating is probably one of the bigger things I notice. Not so much like I can’t think at all, but it’s like my attention gets pulled off track really easily, even when I’m trying to stay with something.\n\nA common example is meetings at work. I’ll be sitting there trying to listen to a budget update, and I’m honestly intending to follow it, but then someone’s phone vibrates, or there are people talking out in the hall, or even some random thought pops in like, “Oh, I forgot to handle something for the league,” and suddenly I’m gone. Not physically, obviously, but mentally I’ve missed the next few minutes. Then I kind of come back and realize everyone else is still on the same topic, but I’ve lost the thread, and I’m trying to piece together what was said without making it obvious.\n\nThat happens with everyday things too, but meetings are where I notice it most because there’s sort of a spotlight on needing to absorb the information. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore people. It’s just one little thing pulls me away, and then it takes effort to get back.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there’s no specific task or responsibility hanging over you or causing stress?\n\nPatient: Right, yeah—I think that’s the part where I’d still say not really, or at least not that I’ve been able to clearly separate out.\n\nI do have times where I feel kind of low or frustrated, but when I look at it closely, it almost always seems tied to something specific—like I’m behind, or I’ve forgotten something, or I’m not keeping up the way I think I should. If you took that pressure away, I don’t think I’d describe myself as just being down for no reason.\n\nAnd with interest in things, I don’t think I’ve lost that. If something is actually enjoyable to me, I can still get into it. It’s more that sometimes I’m distracted or I have a hard time settling into it because something else is in the back of my mind. But I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped enjoying things generally.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that happening when there isn’t some specific stressor attached to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there was no specific task or stressor involved?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you’re asking. I mean, I have off days like anyone does, where I’m irritated or kind of flat, but when I try to think of examples, there’s usually some reason I can point to. It’s not like I wake up and everything feels pointless for no reason, or I suddenly don’t care about things I normally like.\n\nIf I’m not enjoying something, it’s usually because my mind is elsewhere or I’m preoccupied with something hanging over me. But if there isn’t a stressor involved, I don’t really notice that kind of down mood or loss of interest. So I’d say no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when nothing specific was bothering you or stressing you out?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that, at least not that I can identify. It feels more tied to when I’m struggling with responsibilities or when I’m stuck and can’t get myself moving. Outside of that, I wouldn’t say I’m constantly feeling hopeless or anything like that. I just get worn down by the cycle, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or having low energy most days, even when you’re not particularly busy or stressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’m tired or low-energy most days in that general sense. I mean, I have days where I feel drained, sure, but it’s usually hard for me to separate whether that’s because I’ve been busy, or because I’ve been stressing over something I’m not getting done, or because I’m just mentally worn out from fighting with myself about it.\n\nIf you mean waking up already exhausted for no clear reason, or feeling physically low-energy all day even when things are calm, I haven’t really noticed that as a constant thing. It’s more like my energy can be there, but getting it pointed in the right direction is the problem. But no, I wouldn’t say I’m just tired most days for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you feel down or lose interest in things you usually enjoy, even when you’re not struggling with tasks or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I have normal ups and downs, and there are times where I’m irritated or disappointed in myself, but when I try to separate that from being behind on something or feeling like I’ve dropped the ball, I’m not sure there’s much there.\n\nLike if it’s a weekend or I’m with family, or I’m doing something I actually want to do, I can still enjoy it. It’s not like everything feels flat all the time. Sometimes I might have trouble fully relaxing because I know there’s something I’ve avoided, and that kind of hangs over me, but that’s different from just not caring about anything or feeling down for no reason.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed losing interest in things I usually enjoy when there isn’t some responsibility or unfinished task weighing on me. At least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or find yourself physically unable to sit still, maybe pacing, fidgeting, or feeling on edge, even when it’s not about a specific task or responsibility?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as physically restless in that way, at least not as the main thing. I mean, I probably fidget some—like shifting in a chair, tapping a pen, that kind of normal stuff—but I don’t usually feel like I’m crawling out of my skin or like I have to pace around for no reason.\n\nIf I’m on edge, it’s usually because there’s something I’m thinking about or something hanging over me, not just this random physical restlessness. Sitting still isn’t usually the problem by itself. It’s more… I don’t know, my mind may be busy, but my body isn’t necessarily needing to move all the time.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no, not really—not in a way I’ve noticed as a regular issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it hard to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions, even about everyday things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, concentrating is probably one of the bigger things I notice. Not so much like I can’t think at all, but it’s like my attention gets pulled off track really easily, even when I’m trying to stay with something.\n\nA common example is meetings at work. I’ll be sitting there trying to listen to a budget update, and I’m honestly intending to follow it, but then someone’s phone vibrates, or there are people talking out in the hall, or even some random thought pops in like, “Oh, I forgot to handle something for the league,” and suddenly I’m gone. Not physically, obviously, but mentally I’ve missed the next few minutes. Then I kind of come back and realize everyone else is still on the same topic, but I’ve lost the thread, and I’m trying to piece together what was said without making it obvious.\n\nThat happens with everyday things too, but meetings are where I notice it most because there’s sort of a spotlight on needing to absorb the information. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore people. It’s just one little thing pulls me away, and then it takes effort to get back.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there’s no specific task or responsibility hanging over you or causing stress?\n\nPatient: Right, yeah—I think that’s the part where I’d still say not really, or at least not that I’ve been able to clearly separate out.\n\nI do have times where I feel kind of low or frustrated, but when I look at it closely, it almost always seems tied to something specific—like I’m behind, or I’ve forgotten something, or I’m not keeping up the way I think I should. If you took that pressure away, I don’t think I’d describe myself as just being down for no reason.\n\nAnd with interest in things, I don’t think I’ve lost that. If something is actually enjoyable to me, I can still get into it. It’s more that sometimes I’m distracted or I have a hard time settling into it because something else is in the back of my mind. But I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped enjoying things generally.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that happening when there isn’t some specific stressor attached to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there was no specific task or stressor involved?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you’re asking. I mean, I have off days like anyone does, where I’m irritated or kind of flat, but when I try to think of examples, there’s usually some reason I can point to. It’s not like I wake up and everything feels pointless for no reason, or I suddenly don’t care about things I normally like.\n\nIf I’m not enjoying something, it’s usually because my mind is elsewhere or I’m preoccupied with something hanging over me. But if there isn’t a stressor involved, I don’t really notice that kind of down mood or loss of interest. So I’d say no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when nothing specific was bothering you or stressing you out?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that, at least not that I can identify. I’ve had stretches where I’m annoyed with myself or kind of worn down, but when I think through it, there’s usually something I can point to—some responsibility I’m behind on, or something I forgot, or just the general feeling that I’m not staying on top of things.\n\nBut a period of several days where I’m just down, or I stop enjoying things, and there’s no obvious reason for it… I don’t really recognize that in myself. If nothing specific is pressing on me, I usually still want to do the things I like. I might be distracted or restless or not fully settled into them, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in them.\n\nSo, as far as I can tell, no—not several days like that without something bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling so restless that you can’t sit still for very long — even when you’re not actively stressed about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, honestly. I don’t think I move more slowly than usual, at least not in a way that someone would point out or that feels unusual to me.\n\nAnd the restlessness part… I mean, I can get fidgety sometimes, but when you say “so restless I can’t sit still,” I don’t know that I’d describe it that strongly, especially if I’m not actively stressed about something. If I’m waiting somewhere or sitting through something long, I might shift around or feel like I want to get up, but I’ve always thought of that as more just being impatient or uncomfortable rather than a major change.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not really, or at least I haven’t clearly noticed either extreme—slowed down or unable to sit still—when nothing stressful is going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it’s not entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “more than usual,” exactly. I do blame myself for things when I feel like I dropped the ball, especially if something was my responsibility and I missed it or didn’t handle it the way I should have. I can replay that in my head and think, “I should’ve caught that,” or “I should’ve done better.”\n\nBut if you mean feeling guilty out of proportion, or blaming myself for things that really weren’t on me, I don’t think that’s been a major thing. I might take something personally for a bit, especially if there’s a misunderstanding or I feel like I inconvenienced someone, but usually once I think it through, I can tell if it wasn’t fully my fault.\n\nSo… I guess I do get frustrated with myself, but I don’t know that I’ve been feeling guilty in some unusual or persistent way. I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve had plenty of times where I’m unmotivated, but when I really look at it, it’s usually connected to something—like I’m putting off a task, or I feel behind, or there’s some pressure I don’t want to deal with. Sometimes I might have trouble fully relaxing because I know there’s something I’ve avoided, and that kind of hangs over me, but that’s different from just not caring about anything or feeling down for no reason.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed losing interest in things I usually enjoy when there isn’t some responsibility or unfinished task weighing on me. At least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or find yourself physically unable to sit still, maybe pacing, fidgeting, or feeling on edge, even when it’s not about a specific task or responsibility?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as physically restless in that way, at least not as the main thing. I mean, I probably fidget some—like shifting in a chair, tapping a pen, that kind of normal stuff—but I don’t usually feel like I’m crawling out of my skin or like I have to pace around for no reason.\n\nIf I’m on edge, it’s usually because there’s something I’m thinking about or something hanging over me, not just this random physical restlessness. Sitting still isn’t usually the problem by itself. It’s more… I don’t know, my mind may be busy, but my body isn’t necessarily needing to move all the time.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no, not really—not in a way I’ve noticed as a regular issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it hard to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions, even about everyday things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, concentrating is probably one of the bigger things I notice. Not so much like I can’t think at all, but it’s like my attention gets pulled off track really easily, even when I’m trying to stay with something.\n\nA common example is meetings at work. I’ll be sitting there trying to listen to a budget update, and I’m honestly intending to follow it, but then someone’s phone vibrates, or there are people talking out in the hall, or even some random thought pops in like, “Oh, I forgot to handle something for the league,” and suddenly I’m gone. Not physically, obviously, but mentally I’ve missed the next few minutes. Then I kind of come back and realize everyone else is still on the same topic, but I’ve lost the thread, and I’m trying to piece together what was said without making it obvious.\n\nThat happens with everyday things too, but meetings are where I notice it most because there’s sort of a spotlight on needing to absorb the information. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore people. It’s just one little thing pulls me away, and then it takes effort to get back.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there’s no specific task or responsibility hanging over you or causing stress?\n\nPatient: Right, yeah—I think that’s the part where I’d still say not really, or at least not that I’ve been able to clearly separate out.\n\nI do have times where I feel kind of low or frustrated, but when I look at it closely, it almost always seems tied to something specific—like I’m behind, or I’ve forgotten something, or I’m not keeping up the way I think I should. If you took that pressure away, I don’t think I’d describe myself as just being down for no reason.\n\nAnd with interest in things, I don’t think I’ve lost that. If something is actually enjoyable to me, I can still get into it. It’s more that sometimes I’m distracted or I have a hard time settling into it because something else is in the back of my mind. But I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped enjoying things generally.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that happening when there isn’t some specific stressor attached to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there was no specific task or stressor involved?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you’re asking. I mean, I have off days like anyone does, where I’m irritated or kind of flat, but when I try to think of examples, there’s usually some reason I can point to. It’s not like I wake up and everything feels pointless for no reason, or I suddenly don’t care about things I normally like.\n\nIf I’m not enjoying something, it’s usually because my mind is elsewhere or I’m preoccupied with something hanging over me. But if there isn’t a stressor involved, I don’t really notice that kind of down mood or loss of interest. So I’d say no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when nothing specific was bothering you or stressing you out?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that, at least not that I can identify. I’ve had stretches where I’m annoyed with myself or kind of worn down, but when I think through it, there’s usually something I can point to—some responsibility I’m behind on, or something I forgot, or just the general feeling that I’m not staying on top of things.\n\nBut a period of several days where I’m just down, or I stop enjoying things, and there’s no obvious reason for it… I don’t really recognize that in myself. If nothing specific is pressing on me, I usually still want to do the things I like. I might be distracted or restless or not fully settled into them, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in them.\n\nSo, as far as I can tell, no—not several days like that without something bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling so restless that you can’t sit still for very long — even when you’re not actively stressed about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, honestly. I don’t think I move more slowly than usual, at least not in a way that someone would point out or that feels unusual to me.\n\nAnd the restlessness part… I mean, I can get fidgety sometimes, but when you say “so restless I can’t sit still,” I don’t know that I’d describe it that strongly, especially if I’m not actively stressed about something. If I’m waiting somewhere or sitting through something long, I might shift around or feel like I want to get up, but I’ve always thought of that as more just being impatient or uncomfortable rather than a major change.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not really, or at least I haven’t clearly noticed either extreme—slowed down or unable to sit still—when nothing stressful is going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it’s not entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “more than usual,” exactly. I do blame myself for things when I feel like I dropped the ball, especially if something was my responsibility and I missed it or didn’t handle it the way I should have. I can replay that in my head and think, “I should’ve caught that,” or “I should’ve done better.”\n\nBut if you mean feeling guilty out of proportion, or blaming myself for things that really weren’t on me, I don’t think that’s been a major thing. I might take something personally for a bit, especially if there’s a misunderstanding or I feel like I inconvenienced someone, but usually once I think it through, I can tell if it wasn’t fully my fault.\n\nSo… I guess I do get frustrated with myself, but I don’t know that I’ve been feeling guilty in some unusual or persistent way. It feels more tied to specific situations than just a general guilt hanging around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt down or unmotivated for no clear reason, even when you weren’t avoiding tasks or dealing with stressors?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve had plenty of times where I’m unmotivated, but when I really look at it, it’s usually connected to something—like I’m putting off a task, or I feel behind, or there’s some pressure I don’t want to deal with. It’s not usually this random mood that just shows up out of nowhere.\n\nIf anything, I might initially think, “Why can’t I get myself moving?” but then if I slow down and trace it back, there’s normally some reason. A deadline, paperwork, something I’ve delayed, or just feeling like I’ve got too many loose ends. When those aren’t there, I don’t really remember having stretches where I felt down or unmotivated for no clear reason.\n\nSo… I’d say no, not in the way you’re asking. It tends to be tied to something specific, even if I don’t recognize it immediately.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t think I move more slowly than usual, at least not in a way that someone would point out or that feels unusual to me.\n\nAnd the restlessness part… I mean, I can get fidgety sometimes, but when you say “so restless I can’t sit still,” I don’t know that I’d describe it that strongly, especially if I’m not actively stressed about something. If I’m waiting somewhere or sitting through something long, I might shift around or feel like I want to get up, but I’ve always thought of that as more just being impatient or uncomfortable rather than a major change.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not really, or at least I haven’t clearly noticed either extreme—slowed down or unable to sit still—when nothing stressful is going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it’s not entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “more than usual,” exactly. I do blame myself for things when I feel like I dropped the ball, especially if something was my responsibility and I missed it or didn’t handle it the way I should have. I can replay that in my head and think, “I should’ve caught that,” or “I should’ve done better.”\n\nBut if you mean feeling guilty out of proportion, or blaming myself for things that really weren’t on me, I don’t think that’s been a major thing. It feels more tied to specific situations than just a general guilt hanging around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt down or unmotivated for no clear reason, even when you weren’t avoiding tasks or dealing with stressors?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve had plenty of times where I’m unmotivated, but when I really look at it, it’s usually connected to something—like I’m putting off a task, or I feel behind, or there’s some pressure I don’t want to deal with.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think this is kind of the same answer as before. I haven’t really noticed a low mood that just shows up out of nowhere when there isn’t something attached to it.

Like, if I’m feeling down or flat, I can usually connect it to something after I think about it for a minute — being behind on something, feeling like I didn’t handle a responsibility well, or just having something hanging over me. But if I picture a day where there’s no particular task I’m avoiding and nothing I’m responsible for that’s weighing on me, I don’t think I typically just feel low for no clear reason.

I mean, I might have normal off days, like anyone does, but not in a way where I’d say, “I’m depressed and I have no idea why.” It usually makes sense in context, even if I don’t realize the context right away.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF48.0, F32.0, F32.1, F43.20\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what’s been troubling you or what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, so… the main thing that brings me in is that I feel like I keep hitting this mental wall with tasks that require real sitting-down brain work. I’m a medical services manager, and there are parts of my job where I can keep moving and handle people and put out fires, and I do okay with that. But when it comes to things like monthly reports, or sitting down to plan out a multi-site health event in a structured way, I just… I stall.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand that the work matters. I absolutely do. Sometimes it’s important, sometimes other people are waiting on it, and I know it needs to get done. But the moment I try to start, it feels like my brain pushes back, like I’m trying to force myself through mud. So I put it off, and then I feel worse because I know I’m putting it off. Then it becomes this whole cycle where the task gets bigger in my head the longer I avoid it.\n\nI guess that’s what’s been bothering me most — not being lazy, exactly, but feeling like anything that takes sustained mental effort is just so much harder for me to start and stay with than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this kind of difficulty with starting and sticking to tasks happens in other areas of your life, like at home or in your personal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely the same kind of thing outside of work too, though the work examples are the clearest because they have deadlines and consequences. But the pattern is really that if something requires me to sit down, think it through, organize it, and stay with it mentally, I sort of freeze up or drift away from it.\n\nLike with those monthly reports or planning a multi-site health event, I know exactly that it matters. I know people may be waiting on me, and I know it’s part of my responsibility. But it feels like there’s this wall before I even start. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sit down and do the first part,” and somehow I’ll keep circling around it instead of actually doing it. And then the longer I put it off, the heavier it feels, so by the time I do try to start, it’s like the task has become twice as big in my head.\n\nSo yes, it’s not just that I dislike paperwork or planning. It’s more that anything requiring sustained, focused mental effort feels unusually hard for me to start and stick with, even when I genuinely care about getting it done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even when it’s not related to work or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I get frustrated and discouraged, definitely, especially when I’m behind on things or I’ve let something sit too long and it’s turned into this bigger mess in my head. That can make me feel pretty bad about myself in the moment.\n\nBut in terms of feeling down or hopeless in a broader way, or losing interest in things I normally enjoy… I don’t know that I’ve noticed that as the main thing. It feels more tied to when I’m struggling with responsibilities or when I’m stuck and can’t get myself moving. Outside of that, I wouldn’t say I’m constantly feeling hopeless or anything like that. I just get worn down by the cycle, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or having low energy most days, even when you’re not particularly busy or stressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’m tired or low-energy most days in that general sense. I mean, I have days where I feel drained, sure, but it’s usually hard for me to separate whether that’s because I’ve been busy, or because I’ve been stressing over something I’m not getting done, or because I’m just mentally worn out from fighting with myself about it.\n\nIf you mean waking up already exhausted for no clear reason, or feeling physically low-energy all day even when things are calm, I haven’t really noticed that as a constant thing. It’s more like my energy can be there, but getting it pointed in the right direction is the problem. But no, I wouldn’t say I’m just tired most days for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Are there times when you feel down or lose interest in things you usually enjoy, even when you’re not struggling with tasks or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I have normal ups and downs, and there are times where I’m irritated or disappointed in myself, but when I try to separate that from being behind on something or feeling like I’ve dropped the ball, I’m not sure there’s much there.\n\nLike if it’s a weekend or I’m with family, or I’m doing something I actually want to do, I can still enjoy it. It’s not like everything feels flat all the time. Sometimes I might have trouble fully relaxing because I know there’s something I’ve avoided, and that kind of hangs over me, but that’s different from just not caring about anything or feeling down for no reason.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed losing interest in things I usually enjoy when there isn’t some responsibility or unfinished task weighing on me. At least not that I can clearly point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel restless or find yourself physically unable to sit still, maybe pacing, fidgeting, or feeling on edge, even when it’s not about a specific task or responsibility?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe myself as physically restless in that way, at least not as the main thing. I mean, I probably fidget some—like shifting in a chair, tapping a pen, that kind of normal stuff—but I don’t usually feel like I’m crawling out of my skin or like I have to pace around for no reason.\n\nIf I’m on edge, it’s usually because there’s something I’m thinking about or something hanging over me, not just this random physical restlessness. Sitting still isn’t usually the problem by itself. It’s more… I don’t know, my mind may be busy, but my body isn’t necessarily needing to move all the time.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no, not really—not in a way I’ve noticed as a regular issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it hard to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions, even about everyday things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, concentrating is probably one of the bigger things I notice. Not so much like I can’t think at all, but it’s like my attention gets pulled off track really easily, even when I’m trying to stay with something.\n\nA common example is meetings at work. I’ll be sitting there trying to listen to a budget update, and I’m honestly intending to follow it, but then someone’s phone vibrates, or there are people talking out in the hall, or even some random thought pops in like, “Oh, I forgot to handle something for the league,” and suddenly I’m gone. Not physically, obviously, but mentally I’ve missed the next few minutes. Then I kind of come back and realize everyone else is still on the same topic, but I’ve lost the thread, and I’m trying to piece together what was said without making it obvious.\n\nThat happens with everyday things too, but meetings are where I notice it most because there’s sort of a spotlight on needing to absorb the information. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore people. It’s just one little thing pulls me away, and then it takes effort to get back.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there’s no specific task or responsibility hanging over you or causing stress?\n\nPatient: Right, yeah—I think that’s the part where I’d still say not really, or at least not that I’ve been able to clearly separate out.\n\nI do have times where I feel kind of low or frustrated, but when I look at it closely, it almost always seems tied to something specific—like I’m behind, or I’ve forgotten something, or I’m not keeping up the way I think I should. If you took that pressure away, I don’t think I’d describe myself as just being down for no reason.\n\nAnd with interest in things, I don’t think I’ve lost that. If something is actually enjoyable to me, I can still get into it. It’s more that sometimes I’m distracted or I have a hard time settling into it because something else is in the back of my mind. But I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped enjoying things generally.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that happening when there isn’t some specific stressor attached to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there was no specific task or stressor involved?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you’re asking. I mean, I have off days like anyone does, where I’m irritated or kind of flat, but when I try to think of examples, there’s usually some reason I can point to. It’s not like I wake up and everything feels pointless for no reason, or I suddenly don’t care about things I normally like.\n\nIf I’m not enjoying something, it’s usually because my mind is elsewhere or I’m preoccupied with something hanging over me. But if there isn’t a stressor involved, I don’t really notice that kind of down mood or loss of interest. So I’d say no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when nothing specific was bothering you or stressing you out?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that, at least not that I can identify. I’ve had stretches where I’m annoyed with myself or kind of worn down, but when I think through it, there’s usually something I can point to—some responsibility I’m behind on, or something I forgot, or just the general feeling that I’m not staying on top of things.\n\nBut a period of several days where I’m just down, or I stop enjoying things, and there’s no obvious reason for it… I don’t really recognize that in myself. Then I kind of come back and realize everyone else is still on the same topic, but I’ve lost the thread, and I’m trying to piece together what was said without making it obvious.\n\nThat happens with everyday things too, but meetings are where I notice it most because there’s sort of a spotlight on needing to absorb the information. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore people. It’s just one little thing pulls me away, and then it takes effort to get back.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there’s no specific task or responsibility hanging over you or causing stress?\n\nPatient: Right, yeah—I think that’s the part where I’d still say not really, or at least not that I’ve been able to clearly separate out.\n\nI do have times where I feel kind of low or frustrated, but when I look at it closely, it almost always seems tied to something specific—like I’m behind, or I’ve forgotten something, or I’m not keeping up the way I think I should. If you took that pressure away, I don’t think I’d describe myself as just being down for no reason.\n\nAnd with interest in things, I don’t think I’ve lost that. If something is actually enjoyable to me, I can still get into it. It’s more that sometimes I’m distracted or I have a hard time settling into it because something else is in the back of my mind. But I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped enjoying things generally.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that happening when there isn’t some specific stressor attached to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there was no specific task or stressor involved?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you’re asking. I mean, I have off days like anyone does, where I’m irritated or kind of flat, but when I try to think of examples, there’s usually some reason I can point to. It’s not like I wake up and everything feels pointless for no reason, or I suddenly don’t care about things I normally like.\n\nIf I’m not enjoying something, it’s usually because my mind is elsewhere or I’m preoccupied with something hanging over me. But if there isn’t a stressor involved, I don’t really notice that kind of down mood or loss of interest. So I’d say no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when nothing specific was bothering you or stressing you out?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that, at least not that I can identify. I’ve had stretches where I’m annoyed with myself or kind of worn down, but when I think through it, there’s usually something I can point to—some responsibility I’m behind on, or something I forgot, or just the general feeling that I’m not staying on top of things.\n\nBut a period of several days where I’m just down, or I stop enjoying things, and there’s no obvious reason for it… I don’t really recognize that in myself. If nothing specific is pressing on me, I usually still want to do the things I like. I might be distracted or restless or not fully settled into them, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in them.\n\nSo, as far as I can tell, no—not several days like that without something bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling so restless that you can’t sit still for very long — even when you’re not actively stressed about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, honestly. I don’t think I move more slowly than usual, at least not in a way that someone would point out or that feels unusual to me.\n\nAnd the restlessness part… I mean, I can get fidgety sometimes, but when you say “so restless I can’t sit still,” I don’t know that I’d describe it that strongly, especially if I’m not actively stressed about something. If I’m waiting somewhere or sitting through something long, I might shift around or feel like I want to get up, but I’ve always thought of that as more just being impatient or uncomfortable rather than a major change.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not really, or at least I haven’t clearly noticed either extreme—slowed down or unable to sit still—when nothing stressful is going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it’s not entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “more than usual,” exactly. I do blame myself for things when I feel like I dropped the ball, especially if something was my responsibility and I missed it or didn’t handle it the way I should have. I can replay that in my head and think, “I should’ve caught that,” or “I should’ve done better.”\n\nBut if you mean feeling guilty out of proportion, or blaming myself for things that really weren’t on me, I don’t think that’s been a major thing. I might take something personally for a bit, especially if there’s a misunderstanding or I feel like I inconvenienced someone, but usually once I think it through, I can tell if it wasn’t fully my fault.\n\nSo… I guess I do get frustrated with myself, but I don’t know that I’ve been feeling guilty in some unusual or persistent way. It feels more tied to specific situations than just a general guilt hanging around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt down or unmotivated for no clear reason, even when you weren’t avoiding tasks or dealing with stressors?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. It’s not usually this random mood that just shows up out of nowhere.\n\nIf anything, I might initially think, “Why can’t I get myself moving?” but then if I slow down and trace it back, there’s normally some reason. I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way, no. Like, if I’m doing something I normally enjoy and it doesn’t feel as enjoyable, there’s usually some reason I can point to — I’m distracted, or I’ve got something hanging over me, or I’m rushing through it instead of actually being present for it.

Sometimes it takes me a little while to get there, if my mind is elsewhere, but it’s not like the pleasure is gone.

I mean, I have days where I feel drained, sure, but it’s usually hard for me to separate whether that’s because I’ve been busy, or because I’ve been stressing over something I’m not getting done, or because I’m just mentally worn out from fighting with myself about it.\n\nIf you mean waking up already exhausted for no clear reason, or feeling physically low-energy all day even when things are calm, I haven’t really noticed that as a constant thing.
