I mean, I probably fidget some—like shifting in a chair, tapping a pen, that kind of normal stuff—but I don’t usually feel like I’m crawling out of my skin or like I have to pace around for no reason.\n\nIf I’m on edge, it’s usually because there’s something I’m thinking about or something hanging over me, not just this random physical restlessness. Sitting still isn’t usually the problem by itself. It’s more… I don’t know, my mind may be busy, but my body isn’t necessarily needing to move all the time.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no, not really—not in a way I’ve noticed as a regular issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it hard to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions, even about everyday things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, concentrating is probably one of the bigger things I notice. Not so much like I can’t think at all, but it’s like my attention gets pulled off track really easily, even when I’m trying to stay with something.\n\nA common example is meetings at work. I’ll be sitting there trying to listen to a budget update, and I’m honestly intending to follow it, but then someone’s phone vibrates, or there are people talking out in the hall, or even some random thought pops in like, “Oh, I forgot to handle something for the league,” and suddenly I’m gone. Not physically, obviously, but mentally I’ve missed the next few minutes. Then I kind of come back and realize everyone else is still on the same topic, but I’ve lost the thread, and I’m trying to piece together what was said without making it obvious.\n\nThat happens with everyday things too, but meetings are where I notice it most because there’s sort of a spotlight on needing to absorb the information. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore people. It’s just one little thing pulls me away, and then it takes effort to get back.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there’s no specific task or responsibility hanging over you or causing stress?\n\nPatient: Right, yeah—I think that’s the part where I’d still say not really, or at least not that I’ve been able to clearly separate out.\n\nI do have times where I feel kind of low or frustrated, but when I look at it closely, it almost always seems tied to something specific—like I’m behind, or I’ve forgotten something, or I’m not keeping up the way I think I should. If you took that pressure away, I don’t think I’d describe myself as just being down for no reason.\n\nAnd with interest in things, I don’t think I’ve lost that. If something is actually enjoyable to me, I can still get into it. It’s more that sometimes I’m distracted or I have a hard time settling into it because something else is in the back of my mind. But I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped enjoying things generally.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that happening when there isn’t some specific stressor attached to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when there was no specific task or stressor involved?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you’re asking. I mean, I have off days like anyone does, where I’m irritated or kind of flat, but when I try to think of examples, there’s usually some reason I can point to. It’s not like I wake up and everything feels pointless for no reason, or I suddenly don’t care about things I normally like.\n\nIf I’m not enjoying something, it’s usually because my mind is elsewhere or I’m preoccupied with something hanging over me. But if there isn’t a stressor involved, I don’t really notice that kind of down mood or loss of interest. So I’d say no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting several days where you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy, even when nothing specific was bothering you or stressing you out?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that, at least not that I can identify. I’ve had stretches where I’m annoyed with myself or kind of worn down, but when I think through it, there’s usually something I can point to—some responsibility I’m behind on, or something I forgot, or just the general feeling that I’m not staying on top of things.\n\nBut a period of several days where I’m just down, or I stop enjoying things, and there’s no obvious reason for it… I don’t really recognize that in myself. If nothing specific is pressing on me, I usually still want to do the things I like. I might be distracted or restless or not fully settled into them, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in them.\n\nSo, as far as I can tell, no—not several days like that without something bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling so restless that you can’t sit still for very long — even when you’re not actively stressed about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that, honestly. I don’t think I move more slowly than usual, at least not in a way that someone would point out or that feels unusual to me.\n\nAnd the restlessness part… I mean, I can get fidgety sometimes, but when you say “so restless I can’t sit still,” I don’t know that I’d describe it that strongly, especially if I’m not actively stressed about something. If I’m waiting somewhere or sitting through something long, I might shift around or feel like I want to get up, but I’ve always thought of that as more just being impatient or uncomfortable rather than a major change.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not really, or at least I haven’t clearly noticed either extreme—slowed down or unable to sit still—when nothing stressful is going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, even when it’s not entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “more than usual,” exactly. I do blame myself for things when I feel like I dropped the ball, especially if something was my responsibility and I missed it or didn’t handle it the way I should have. I can replay that in my head and think, “I should’ve caught that,” or “I should’ve done better.”\n\nBut if you mean feeling guilty out of proportion, or blaming myself for things that really weren’t on me, I don’t think that’s been a major thing. I might take something personally for a bit, especially if there’s a misunderstanding or I feel like I inconvenienced someone, but usually once I think it through, I can tell if it wasn’t fully my fault.\n\nSo… I guess I do get frustrated with myself, but I don’t know that I’ve been feeling guilty in some unusual or persistent way. It feels more tied to specific situations than just a general guilt hanging around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt down or unmotivated for no clear reason, even when you weren’t avoiding tasks or dealing with stressors?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ve had plenty of times where I’m unmotivated, but when I really look at it, it’s usually connected to something—like I’m putting off a task, or I feel behind, or there’s some pressure I don’t want to deal with. It’s not usually this random mood that just shows up out of nowhere.\n\nIf anything, I might initially think, “Why can’t I get myself moving?” but then if I slow down and trace it back, there’s normally some reason. A deadline, paperwork, something I’ve delayed, or just feeling like I’ve got too many loose ends. When those aren’t there, I don’t really remember having stretches where I felt down or unmotivated for no clear reason.\n\nSo… I’d say no, not in the way you’re asking. It tends to be tied to something specific, even if I don’t recognize it immediately.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed yourself feeling down or in a low mood for no clear reason, even when you’re not dealing with any tasks or responsibilities that might explain it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think this is kind of the same answer as before. I haven’t really noticed a low mood that just shows up out of nowhere when there isn’t something attached to it.\n\nLike, if I’m feeling down or flat, I can usually connect it to something after I think about it for a minute — being behind on something, feeling like I didn’t handle a responsibility well, or just having something hanging over me. But if I picture a day where there’s no particular task I’m avoiding and nothing I’m responsible for that’s weighing on me, I don’t think I typically just feel low for no clear reason.\n\nI mean, I might have normal off days, like anyone does, but not in a way where I’d say, “I’m depressed and I have no idea why.” It usually makes sense in context, even if I don’t realize the context right away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found that activities you used to enjoy no longer give you the same sense of pleasure, even when you’re not dealing with any stressors or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way, no. Like, if I’m doing something I normally enjoy and it doesn’t feel as enjoyable, there’s usually some reason I can point to — I’m distracted, or I’ve got something hanging over me, or I’m rushing through it instead of actually being present for it.\n\nBut if you mean, like, things I used to like just stopped feeling good even when everything else is basically fine… I don’t think so.
